========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, what brings me in is… it’s kind of embarrassing, actually. I’ve been noticing that when people are talking directly to me, like at the museum when we’re discussing the next art walk, I’ll be looking right at them and nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I missed the whole middle part of what they said. Not because I don’t care—I do care—but it’s like my mind slips off somewhere for a moment.

Then I have to ask them to repeat it, like, “Could you run that by me again?” And after a while I feel awkward, because it’s not like they were across the room or mumbling. They were right there in front of me. So I guess I’m here because that keeps happening, and I’d like to understand why.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I haven’t really sorted out whether it’s stress exactly. It happens in pretty ordinary situations too, like at the museum when we’re talking about the next art walk. Someone will be speaking right to me, and I’ll be looking at them, nodding like I’m following, and then all of a sudden I realize I didn’t catch the middle of what they said.

And then I have to say, “Sorry, could you run that by me again?” It’s not that I’m panicking in that moment or anything. It’s more like my attention just sort of drifts off without me noticing, and by the time I notice, I’ve missed a chunk of it. So stress might make it worse, maybe, but I can’t say it only happens when I’m anxious.

It’s more like my attention just sort of drifts off without me noticing, and by the time I notice, I’ve missed a chunk of it. So stress might make it worse, maybe, but I can’t say it only happens when I’m anxious.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure how to separate that out, to be honest. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who just can’t remember the day or anything like that. If something happened earlier, I usually have some sense of it.

But with things people tell me… that’s harder to answer, because sometimes if I don’t remember it later, I don’t always know whether I forgot it after hearing it, or whether I never really took it in properly in the first place. Like, if someone says, “Remember I told you this?” I may think, well, maybe you did, but maybe that was one of those times where I was looking like I was listening and I missed part of it.

So outside of those moments, I haven’t especially noticed a separate memory problem, if that makes sense. It feels more like the trouble starts at the point of taking in what’s being said.

Someone will be speaking right to me, and I’ll be looking at them, nodding like I’m following, and then all of a sudden I realize I didn’t catch the middle of what they said.\n\nAnd then I have to say, “Sorry, could you run that by me again?” It’s not that I’m panicking in that moment or anything. It’s more like my attention just sort of drifts off without me noticing, and by the time I notice, I’ve missed a chunk of it. So stress might make it worse, maybe, but I can’t say it only happens when I’m anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened earlier in the day or things that people told you outside of these moments when your mind slips away?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate that out, to be honest. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who just can’t remember the day or anything like that. If something happened earlier, I usually have some sense of it.\n\nBut with things people tell me… that’s harder to answer, because sometimes if I don’t remember it later, I don’t always know whether I forgot it after hearing it, or whether I never really took it in properly in the first place. Like, if someone says, “Remember I told you this?” I may think, well, maybe you did, but maybe that was one of those times where I was looking like I was listening and I missed part of it.\n\nSo outside of those moments, I haven’t especially noticed a separate memory problem, if that makes sense. It feels more like the trouble starts at the point of taking in what’s being said.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel anything physically, like a sensation in your body, when your mind slips away and you miss part of what someone is saying?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 63 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. It’s not like I feel dizzy or my heart starts racing or I get a tightness in my chest right when it happens. At least, I haven’t connected it with anything like that.

It’s more that afterward I sort of “come back,” if that’s the right way to put it, and realize I’ve missed something. Then I feel awkward or a little embarrassed because the person was talking right to me and I was acting like I was following. But physically, during the slipping-away part, I can’t say there’s a specific sensation I recognize. If there is, it’s subtle enough that I haven’t really picked up on it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that happens too. Reading is probably the easiest example for me to notice, because there’s a page right in front of me showing that I didn’t really take it in.

Like, if I sit down with a gallery packet, or some notes about an exhibit or an upcoming art walk, I’ll start out okay. I’ll be reading the first paragraph or two and thinking, alright, I’m following this. But then after a few minutes my eyes are still going across the page, line by line, and it looks like I’m reading, but my mind has gone somewhere else. Then I get to the bottom of a section and realize I don’t actually know what I just read.

So I’ll go back and read the same part again. Sometimes I have to do that more than once, which gets a little frustrating because it’s not usually that the material is impossible or anything. It’s more like I didn’t stay with it long enough for it to land. Watching something can be similar, but with reading I notice it more clearly because I catch myself rereading the same few lines.

Someone will be speaking right to me, and I’ll be looking at them, nodding like I’m following, and then all of a sudden I realize I didn’t catch the middle of what they said.\n\nAnd then I have to say, “Sorry, could you run that by me again?” It’s not that I’m panicking in that moment or anything. It’s more like my attention just sort of drifts off without me noticing, and by the time I notice, I’ve missed a chunk of it. So stress might make it worse, maybe, but I can’t say it only happens when I’m anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened earlier in the day or things that people told you outside of these moments when your mind slips away?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate that out, to be honest. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who just can’t remember the day or anything like that. If something happened earlier, I usually have some sense of it.\n\nBut with things people tell me… that’s harder to answer, because sometimes if I don’t remember it later, I don’t always know whether I forgot it after hearing it, or whether I never really took it in properly in the first place. Like, if someone says, “Remember I told you this?” I may think, well, maybe you did, but maybe that was one of those times where I was looking like I was listening and I missed part of it.\n\nSo outside of those moments, I haven’t especially noticed a separate memory problem, if that makes sense. It feels more like the trouble starts at the point of taking in what’s being said.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like a sensation in your body, when your mind slips away and you miss part of what someone is saying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. It’s not like I feel dizzy or my heart starts racing or I get a tightness in my chest right when it happens. At least, I haven’t connected it with anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that afterward I sort of “come back,” if that’s the right way to put it, and realize I’ve missed something. Then I feel awkward or a little embarrassed because the person was talking right to me and I was acting like I was following. But physically, during the slipping-away part, I can’t say there’s a specific sensation I recognize. If there is, it’s subtle enough that I haven’t really picked up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself zoning out or missing parts of what you're reading or watching, similar to what happens when you're listening to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens too. Reading is probably the easiest example for me to notice, because there’s a page right in front of me showing that I didn’t really take it in.\n\nLike, if I sit down with a gallery packet, or some notes about an exhibit or an upcoming art walk, I’ll start out okay. I’ll be reading the first paragraph or two and thinking, alright, I’m following this. But then after a few minutes my eyes are still going across the page, line by line, and it looks like I’m reading, but my mind has gone somewhere else. Then I get to the bottom of a section and realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I’ll go back and read the same part again. Sometimes I have to do that more than once, which gets a little frustrating because it’s not usually that the material is impossible or anything. It’s more like I didn’t stay with it long enough for it to land. Watching something can be similar, but with reading I notice it more clearly because I catch myself rereading the same few lines.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do, especially with things like reading through a gallery packet or a set of notes where I really do need to take it in. I’ll sit down and start out perfectly fine — like the first little bit, I’m with it, I understand what I’m reading, I know why I’m looking at it.

But then after a few minutes, it’s like my eyes keep doing the job but my mind doesn’t. I’m still moving across the page, line by line, and if someone looked at me they’d probably think I was reading carefully. But then I get to the end of a paragraph or a section and realize, wait, I don’t actually know what I just read.

So I have to go back and read the same part again, sometimes more than once. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid it, and it’s not that I can’t understand the material. It’s more like my attention just doesn’t stay attached to it long enough.

It’s more like my attention just sort of drifts off without me noticing, and by the time I notice, I’ve missed a chunk of it. Like, if someone says, “Remember I told you this?” I may think, well, maybe you did, but maybe that was one of those times where I was looking like I was listening and I missed part of it.\n\nSo outside of those moments, I haven’t especially noticed a separate memory problem, if that makes sense. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really right before, no. I wouldn’t say I feel anxious first and then drift off. It’s more the other way around, I think — I’m at the museum, someone is talking directly to me about the next art walk or something we’re planning, and I’m looking at them like I’m listening. I’m not upset in that moment, not that I can tell.

Then all of a sudden I realize I missed the middle of what they said. Like I caught the beginning, and maybe I catch the end, but there’s this gap where I don’t really know how we got from one point to the next. That’s when I get uncomfortable, because I have to say, “I’m sorry, can you run that by me again?” And I don’t like doing that, especially if they were explaining something pretty straightforward.

So the anxious or embarrassed feeling is more after I notice I missed it, not before. Beforehand it just kind of happens.

They were right there in front of me. It happens in pretty ordinary situations too, like at the museum when we’re talking about the next art walk. Someone will be speaking right to me, and I’ll be looking at them, nodding like I’m following, and then all of a sudden I realize I didn’t catch the middle of what they said.\n\nAnd then I have to say, “Sorry, could you run that by me again?” It’s not that I’m panicking in that moment or anything. It’s more like my attention just sort of drifts off without me noticing, and by the time I notice, I’ve missed a chunk of it. So stress might make it worse, maybe, but I can’t say it only happens when I’m anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened earlier in the day or things that people told you outside of these moments when your mind slips away?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate that out, to be honest. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who just can’t remember the day or anything like that. If something happened earlier, I usually have some sense of it.\n\nBut with things people tell me… that’s harder to answer, because sometimes if I don’t remember it later, I don’t always know whether I forgot it after hearing it, or whether I never really took it in properly in the first place. Like, if someone says, “Remember I told you this?” I may think, well, maybe you did, but maybe that was one of those times where I was looking like I was listening and I missed part of it.\n\nSo outside of those moments, I haven’t especially noticed a separate memory problem, if that makes sense. It feels more like the trouble starts at the point of taking in what’s being said.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like a sensation in your body, when your mind slips away and you miss part of what someone is saying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. Reading is probably the easiest example for me to notice, because there’s a page right in front of me showing that I didn’t really take it in.\n\nLike, if I sit down with a gallery packet, or some notes about an exhibit or an upcoming art walk, I’ll start out okay. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely when I’m alone too. In some ways it’s easier to notice then, because there isn’t another person there keeping me on track.

Like if I sit down with a gallery packet or a set of notes, I can start out fine. I’ll think, okay, I’m just going to read through this and get what I need from it. And for the first few minutes I usually do. But then my eyes are still moving over the page, like I’m reading each line, and my mind has kind of wandered off somewhere else without me realizing it.

Then I get to the end of a paragraph or a section and I have that moment of, “Wait, what did I just read?” And I’ll have to go back and read the same part again. Sometimes it’s not even that the material is hard — it’s just that I didn’t really stay with it. That can happen even when I’m by myself and there’s nothing especially dramatic going on around me.

It’s more like my attention just doesn’t stay attached to it long enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset right before your mind slips away and you miss part of what someone is saying?\n\nPatient: Not really right before, no. I wouldn’t say I feel anxious first and then drift off. It’s more the other way around, I think — I’m at the museum, someone is talking directly to me about the next art walk or something we’re planning, and I’m looking at them like I’m listening. I’m not upset in that moment, not that I can tell.\n\nThen all of a sudden I realize I missed the middle of what they said. Like I caught the beginning, and maybe I catch the end, but there’s this gap where I don’t really know how we got from one point to the next. That’s when I get uncomfortable, because I have to say, “I’m sorry, can you run that by me again?” And I don’t like doing that, especially if they were explaining something pretty straightforward.\n\nSo the anxious or embarrassed feeling is more after I notice I missed it, not before. Beforehand it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're alone, like when you're trying to work on a project by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely when I’m alone too. Yes… I think it has, though I don’t always like admitting that, because I’ve been involved with the museum group for a long time and I used to feel pretty steady with it.

It’s not like anyone has sat me down and said, “You can’t handle this,” or anything harsh like that. It’s been more subtle. Some of the planning pieces — like keeping track of what needs to be discussed for an art walk, or following through on little details from a meeting — those have sort of quietly shifted away from me. Someone else will take the lead on that part now, or they’ll say, “Oh, I’ve got this,” and I can tell it’s partly because I’ve missed things or needed things repeated.

And that does affect how I feel leading the art walks. I used to feel confident being out front, talking through the pieces and keeping the flow going. Now I second-guess myself more. I worry I’m going to miss something someone told me, or forget part of the plan, or not be as on top of it as I should be. So I still care about it, very much, but I don’t feel as reliable in that role as I used to.

Like, if someone says, “Remember I told you this?” I may think, well, maybe you did, but maybe that was one of those times where I was looking like I was listening and I missed part of it.\n\nSo outside of those moments, I haven’t especially noticed a separate memory problem, if that makes sense. It feels more like the trouble starts at the point of taking in what’s being said.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like a sensation in your body, when your mind slips away and you miss part of what someone is saying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. It’s not like I feel dizzy or my heart starts racing or I get a tightness in my chest right when it happens. At least, I haven’t connected it with anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that afterward I sort of “come back,” if that’s the right way to put it, and realize I’ve missed something. Then I feel awkward or a little embarrassed because the person was talking right to me and I was acting like I was following. But physically, during the slipping-away part, I can’t say there’s a specific sensation I recognize. If there is, it’s subtle enough that I haven’t really picked up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself zoning out or missing parts of what you're reading or watching, similar to what happens when you're listening to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens too. Reading is probably the easiest example for me to notice, because there’s a page right in front of me showing that I didn’t really take it in.\n\nLike, if I sit down with a gallery packet, or some notes about an exhibit or an upcoming art walk, I’ll start out okay. I’ll be reading the first paragraph or two and thinking, alright, I’m following this. But then after a few minutes my eyes are still going across the page, line by line, and it looks like I’m reading, but my mind has gone somewhere else. Then I get to the bottom of a section and realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I’ll go back and read the same part again. Sometimes I have to do that more than once, which gets a little frustrating because it’s not usually that the material is impossible or anything. It’s more like I didn’t stay with it long enough for it to land. Watching something can be similar, but with reading I notice it more clearly because I catch myself rereading the same few lines.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself zoning out or losing focus when you're doing tasks that require your full attention, like working on a project or during conversations with multiple people?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, especially with things like reading through a gallery packet or a set of notes where I really do need to take it in. I’ll sit down and start out perfectly fine — like the first little bit, I’m with it, I understand what I’m reading, I know why I’m looking at it.\n\nBut then after a few minutes, it’s like my eyes keep doing the job but my mind doesn’t. I’m still moving across the page, line by line, and if someone looked at me they’d probably think I was reading carefully. But then I get to the end of a paragraph or a section and realize, wait, I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I have to go back and read the same part again, sometimes more than once. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid it, and it’s not that I can’t understand the material. It’s more like my attention just doesn’t stay attached to it long enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset right before your mind slips away and you miss part of what someone is saying?\n\nPatient: Not really right before, no. I wouldn’t say I feel anxious first and then drift off. It’s more the other way around, I think — I’m at the museum, someone is talking directly to me about the next art walk or something we’re planning, and I’m looking at them like I’m listening. I’m not upset in that moment, not that I can tell.\n\nThen all of a sudden I realize I missed the middle of what they said. Like I caught the beginning, and maybe I catch the end, but there’s this gap where I don’t really know how we got from one point to the next. That’s when I get uncomfortable, because I have to say, “I’m sorry, can you run that by me again?” And I don’t like doing that, especially if they were explaining something pretty straightforward.\n\nSo the anxious or embarrassed feeling is more after I notice I missed it, not before. Beforehand it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're alone, like when you're trying to work on a project by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely when I’m alone too. In some ways it’s easier to notice then, because there isn’t another person there keeping me on track.\n\nLike if I sit down with a gallery packet or a set of notes, I can start out fine. I’ll think, okay, I’m just going to read through this and get what I need from it. And for the first few minutes I usually do. But then my eyes are still moving over the page, like I’m reading each line, and my mind has kind of wandered off somewhere else without me realizing it.\n\nThen I get to the end of a paragraph or a section and I have that moment of, “Wait, what did I just read?” And I’ll have to go back and read the same part again. Sometimes it’s not even that the material is hard — it’s just that I didn’t really stay with it. That can happen even when I’m by myself and there’s nothing especially dramatic going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these episodes of zoning out or missing parts of conversations are affecting your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I don’t always like admitting that, because I’ve been involved with the museum group for a long time and I used to feel pretty steady with it.\n\nIt’s not like anyone has sat me down and said, “You can’t handle this,” or anything harsh like that. It’s been more subtle. Some of the planning pieces — like keeping track of what needs to be discussed for an art walk, or following through on little details from a meeting — those have sort of quietly shifted away from me. Someone else will take the lead on that part now, or they’ll say, “Oh, I’ve got this,” and I can tell it’s partly because I’ve missed things or needed things repeated.\n\nAnd that does affect how I feel leading the art walks. I used to feel confident being out front, talking through the pieces and keeping the flow going. Now I second-guess myself more. I worry I’m going to miss something someone told me, or forget part of the plan, or not be as on top of it as I should be. So I still care about it, very much, but I don’t feel as reliable in that role as I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F43.8", "F88"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It bothers me, yes. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart over it every day, but it does upset me more than I probably let on.

The hardest part is that it’s changed how I feel in the museum group. That’s been a place where I used to feel useful and, you know, like I had my part. But because I miss pieces of conversations or don’t catch all the details, some of the planning things have sort of been moved away from me. Not in a mean way — nobody has been cruel about it — but I notice it. Someone else will step in to handle a detail, or they’ll kind of take over the organizing part, and I’m left thinking, “Well, they probably don’t trust me with that like they used to.”

And leading the art walks… that’s where I feel it emotionally. I used to feel pretty confident doing that. Now I get this nagging feeling that I’m going to miss something important or not be as sharp as I should be. So the upset is not constant panic or anything like that, but it’s discouraging. It makes me feel less capable than I used to feel.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F88\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is… it’s kind of embarrassing, actually. I’ve been noticing that when people are talking directly to me, like at the museum when we’re discussing the next art walk, I’ll be looking right at them and nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I missed the whole middle part of what they said. Not because I don’t care—I do care—but it’s like my mind slips off somewhere for a moment.\n\nThen I have to ask them to repeat it, like, “Could you run that by me again?” And after a while I feel awkward, because it’s not like they were across the room or mumbling. They were right there in front of me. So I guess I’m here because that keeps happening, and I’d like to understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do these moments where your mind slips away tend to happen more often when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I mean… I haven’t really sorted out whether it’s stress exactly. It happens in pretty ordinary situations too, like at the museum when we’re talking about the next art walk. Someone will be speaking right to me, and I’ll be looking at them, nodding like I’m following, and then all of a sudden I realize I didn’t catch the middle of what they said.\n\nAnd then I have to say, “Sorry, could you run that by me again?” It’s not that I’m panicking in that moment or anything. It’s more like my attention just sort of drifts off without me noticing, and by the time I notice, I’ve missed a chunk of it. So stress might make it worse, maybe, but I can’t say it only happens when I’m anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened earlier in the day or things that people told you outside of these moments when your mind slips away?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate that out, to be honest. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who just can’t remember the day or anything like that. If something happened earlier, I usually have some sense of it.\n\nBut with things people tell me… that’s harder to answer, because sometimes if I don’t remember it later, I don’t always know whether I forgot it after hearing it, or whether I never really took it in properly in the first place. Like, if someone says, “Remember I told you this?” I may think, well, maybe you did, but maybe that was one of those times where I was looking like I was listening and I missed part of it.\n\nSo outside of those moments, I haven’t especially noticed a separate memory problem, if that makes sense. It feels more like the trouble starts at the point of taking in what’s being said.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like a sensation in your body, when your mind slips away and you miss part of what someone is saying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. It’s not like I feel dizzy or my heart starts racing or I get a tightness in my chest right when it happens. At least, I haven’t connected it with anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that afterward I sort of “come back,” if that’s the right way to put it, and realize I’ve missed something. Then I feel awkward or a little embarrassed because the person was talking right to me and I was acting like I was following. But physically, during the slipping-away part, I can’t say there’s a specific sensation I recognize. If there is, it’s subtle enough that I haven’t really picked up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself zoning out or missing parts of what you're reading or watching, similar to what happens when you're listening to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens too. Reading is probably the easiest example for me to notice, because there’s a page right in front of me showing that I didn’t really take it in.\n\nLike, if I sit down with a gallery packet, or some notes about an exhibit or an upcoming art walk, I’ll start out okay. I’ll be reading the first paragraph or two and thinking, alright, I’m following this. But then after a few minutes my eyes are still going across the page, line by line, and it looks like I’m reading, but my mind has gone somewhere else. Then I get to the bottom of a section and realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I’ll go back and read the same part again. Sometimes I have to do that more than once, which gets a little frustrating because it’s not usually that the material is impossible or anything. It’s more like I didn’t stay with it long enough for it to land. Watching something can be similar, but with reading I notice it more clearly because I catch myself rereading the same few lines.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself zoning out or losing focus when you're doing tasks that require your full attention, like working on a project or during conversations with multiple people?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, especially with things like reading through a gallery packet or a set of notes where I really do need to take it in. I’ll sit down and start out perfectly fine — like the first little bit, I’m with it, I understand what I’m reading, I know why I’m looking at it.\n\nBut then after a few minutes, it’s like my eyes keep doing the job but my mind doesn’t. I’m still moving across the page, line by line, and if someone looked at me they’d probably think I was reading carefully. But then I get to the end of a paragraph or a section and realize, wait, I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I have to go back and read the same part again, sometimes more than once. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid it, and it’s not that I can’t understand the material. It’s more like my attention just doesn’t stay attached to it long enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset right before your mind slips away and you miss part of what someone is saying?\n\nPatient: Not really right before, no. I wouldn’t say I feel anxious first and then drift off. It’s more the other way around, I think — I’m at the museum, someone is talking directly to me about the next art walk or something we’re planning, and I’m looking at them like I’m listening. I’m not upset in that moment, not that I can tell.\n\nThen all of a sudden I realize I missed the middle of what they said. Like I caught the beginning, and maybe I catch the end, but there’s this gap where I don’t really know how we got from one point to the next. That’s when I get uncomfortable, because I have to say, “I’m sorry, can you run that by me again?” And I don’t like doing that, especially if they were explaining something pretty straightforward.\n\nSo the anxious or embarrassed feeling is more after I notice I missed it, not before. Beforehand it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're alone, like when you're trying to work on a project by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely when I’m alone too. In some ways it’s easier to notice then, because there isn’t another person there keeping me on track.\n\nLike if I sit down with a gallery packet or a set of notes, I can start out fine. I’ll think, okay, I’m just going to read through this and get what I need from it. And for the first few minutes I usually do. But then my eyes are still moving over the page, like I’m reading each line, and my mind has kind of wandered off somewhere else without me realizing it.\n\nThen I get to the end of a paragraph or a section and I have that moment of, “Wait, what did I just read?” And I’ll have to go back and read the same part again. Sometimes it’s not even that the material is hard — it’s just that I didn’t really stay with it. That can happen even when I’m by myself and there’s nothing especially dramatic going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these episodes of zoning out or missing parts of conversations are affecting your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I don’t always like admitting that, because I’ve been involved with the museum group for a long time and I used to feel pretty steady with it.\n\nIt’s not like anyone has sat me down and said, “You can’t handle this,” or anything harsh like that. It’s been more subtle. Some of the planning pieces — like keeping track of what needs to be discussed for an art walk, or following through on little details from a meeting — those have sort of quietly shifted away from me. Someone else will take the lead on that part now, or they’ll say, “Oh, I’ve got this,” and I can tell it’s partly because I’ve missed things or needed things repeated.\n\nAnd that does affect how I feel leading the art walks. I used to feel confident being out front, talking through the pieces and keeping the flow going. Now I second-guess myself more. I worry I’m going to miss something someone told me, or forget part of the plan, or not be as on top of it as I should be. So I still care about it, very much, but I don’t feel as reliable in that role as I used to.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or upset do you feel about these episodes of zoning out and missing parts of conversations?\n\nPatient: It bothers me, yes. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart over it every day, but it does upset me more than I probably let on.\n\nThe hardest part is that it’s changed how I feel in the museum group. That’s been a place where I used to feel useful and, you know, like I had my part. But because I miss pieces of conversations or don’t catch all the details, some of the planning things have sort of been moved away from me. Not in a mean way — nobody has been cruel about it — but I notice it. Someone else will step in to handle a detail, or they’ll kind of take over the organizing part, and I’m left thinking, “Well, they probably don’t trust me with that like they used to.”\n\nAnd leading the art walks… that’s where I feel it emotionally. I used to feel pretty confident doing that. Now I get this nagging feeling that I’m going to miss something important or not be as sharp as I should be. So the upset is not constant panic or anything like that, but it’s discouraging. It makes me feel less capable than I used to feel.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

That can happen even when I’m by myself and there’s nothing especially dramatic going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these episodes of zoning out or missing parts of conversations are affecting your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I don’t always like admitting that, because I’ve been involved with the museum group for a long time and I used to feel pretty steady with it.\n\nIt’s not like anyone has sat me down and said, “You can’t handle this,” or anything harsh like that. It’s been more subtle. Some of the planning pieces — like keeping track of what needs to be discussed for an art walk, or following through on little details from a meeting — those have sort of quietly shifted away from me. Someone else will take the lead on that part now, or they’ll say, “Oh, I’ve got this,” and I can tell it’s partly because I’ve missed things or needed things repeated.\n\nAnd that does affect how I feel leading the art walks. I used to feel confident being out front, talking through the pieces and keeping the flow going. Now I second-guess myself more. I worry I’m going to miss something someone told me, or forget part of the plan, or not be as on top of it as I should be. So I still care about it, very much, but I don’t feel as reliable in that role as I used to.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or upset do you feel about these episodes of zoning out and missing parts of conversations?\n\nPatient: It bothers me, yes. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart over it every day, but it does upset me more than I probably let on.\n\nThe hardest part is that it’s changed how I feel in the museum group. That’s been a place where I used to feel useful and, you know, like I had my part. But because I miss pieces of conversations or don’t catch all the details, some of the planning things have sort of been moved away from me. Not in a mean way — nobody has been cruel about it — but I notice it. Someone else will step in to handle a detail, or they’ll kind of take over the organizing part, and I’m left thinking, “Well, they probably don’t trust me with that like they used to.”\n\nAnd leading the art walks… that’s where I feel it emotionally. I used to feel pretty confident doing that. Now I get this nagging feeling that I’m going to miss something important or not be as sharp as I should be. So the upset is not constant panic or anything like that, but it’s discouraging. It makes me feel less capable than I used to feel.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you having trouble completing everyday tasks because of these episodes of zoning out?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, some. Not every single day, and I do eventually get things done most of the time, but the follow-through is where I notice it.

For example, with the volunteer group, someone might give me a few things to handle before an art walk. And I’ll start out doing it — like I’ll print the maps, and I’ll feel like, okay, I’m on it. But then the next part, like calling the museum desk or making sure the folders are completely ready, can just sort of slip away from me. It’s not that I decided not to do it. It’s more like I did the first piece and my brain treated that as if the whole task was finished.

Then later someone will ask, “Did you call the desk?” or “Are the folders ready?” and I’ll realize they’re only half-ready, or I never made the call. That’s embarrassing, because these are ordinary things. They’re not complicated. But if there are several steps, especially if nobody checks in, I can lose track after the first part and need a reminder.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F88\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is… it’s kind of embarrassing, actually. I’ve been noticing that when people are talking directly to me, like at the museum when we’re discussing the next art walk, I’ll be looking right at them and nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I missed the whole middle part of what they said. Not because I don’t care—I do care—but it’s like my mind slips off somewhere for a moment.\n\nThen I have to ask them to repeat it, like, “Could you run that by me again?” And after a while I feel awkward, because it’s not like they were across the room or mumbling. They were right there in front of me. So I guess I’m here because that keeps happening, and I’d like to understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do these moments where your mind slips away tend to happen more often when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I mean… I haven’t really sorted out whether it’s stress exactly. It happens in pretty ordinary situations too, like at the museum when we’re talking about the next art walk. Someone will be speaking right to me, and I’ll be looking at them, nodding like I’m following, and then all of a sudden I realize I didn’t catch the middle of what they said.\n\nAnd then I have to say, “Sorry, could you run that by me again?” It’s not that I’m panicking in that moment or anything. It’s more like my attention just sort of drifts off without me noticing, and by the time I notice, I’ve missed a chunk of it. So stress might make it worse, maybe, but I can’t say it only happens when I’m anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened earlier in the day or things that people told you outside of these moments when your mind slips away?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate that out, to be honest. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who just can’t remember the day or anything like that. If something happened earlier, I usually have some sense of it.\n\nBut with things people tell me… that’s harder to answer, because sometimes if I don’t remember it later, I don’t always know whether I forgot it after hearing it, or whether I never really took it in properly in the first place. Like, if someone says, “Remember I told you this?” I may think, well, maybe you did, but maybe that was one of those times where I was looking like I was listening and I missed part of it.\n\nSo outside of those moments, I haven’t especially noticed a separate memory problem, if that makes sense. It feels more like the trouble starts at the point of taking in what’s being said.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like a sensation in your body, when your mind slips away and you miss part of what someone is saying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. It’s not like I feel dizzy or my heart starts racing or I get a tightness in my chest right when it happens. At least, I haven’t connected it with anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that afterward I sort of “come back,” if that’s the right way to put it, and realize I’ve missed something. Then I feel awkward or a little embarrassed because the person was talking right to me and I was acting like I was following. But physically, during the slipping-away part, I can’t say there’s a specific sensation I recognize. If there is, it’s subtle enough that I haven’t really picked up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself zoning out or missing parts of what you're reading or watching, similar to what happens when you're listening to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens too. Reading is probably the easiest example for me to notice, because there’s a page right in front of me showing that I didn’t really take it in.\n\nLike, if I sit down with a gallery packet, or some notes about an exhibit or an upcoming art walk, I’ll start out okay. I’ll be reading the first paragraph or two and thinking, alright, I’m following this. But then after a few minutes my eyes are still going across the page, line by line, and it looks like I’m reading, but my mind has gone somewhere else. Then I get to the bottom of a section and realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I’ll go back and read the same part again. Sometimes I have to do that more than once, which gets a little frustrating because it’s not usually that the material is impossible or anything. It’s more like I didn’t stay with it long enough for it to land. Watching something can be similar, but with reading I notice it more clearly because I catch myself rereading the same few lines.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself zoning out or losing focus when you're doing tasks that require your full attention, like working on a project or during conversations with multiple people?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, especially with things like reading through a gallery packet or a set of notes where I really do need to take it in. I’ll sit down and start out perfectly fine — like the first little bit, I’m with it, I understand what I’m reading, I know why I’m looking at it.\n\nBut then after a few minutes, it’s like my eyes keep doing the job but my mind doesn’t. I’m still moving across the page, line by line, and if someone looked at me they’d probably think I was reading carefully. But then I get to the end of a paragraph or a section and realize, wait, I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I have to go back and read the same part again, sometimes more than once. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid it, and it’s not that I can’t understand the material. It’s more like my attention just doesn’t stay attached to it long enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset right before your mind slips away and you miss part of what someone is saying?\n\nPatient: Not really right before, no. I wouldn’t say I feel anxious first and then drift off. It’s more the other way around, I think — I’m at the museum, someone is talking directly to me about the next art walk or something we’re planning, and I’m looking at them like I’m listening. I’m not upset in that moment, not that I can tell.\n\nThen all of a sudden I realize I missed the middle of what they said. Like I caught the beginning, and maybe I catch the end, but there’s this gap where I don’t really know how we got from one point to the next. That’s when I get uncomfortable, because I have to say, “I’m sorry, can you run that by me again?” And I don’t like doing that, especially if they were explaining something pretty straightforward.\n\nSo the anxious or embarrassed feeling is more after I notice I missed it, not before. Beforehand it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're alone, like when you're trying to work on a project by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely when I’m alone too. But then my eyes are still moving over the page, like I’m reading each line, and my mind has kind of wandered off somewhere else without me realizing it.\n\nThen I get to the end of a paragraph or a section and I have that moment of, “Wait, what did I just read?” And I’ll have to go back and read the same part again. Sometimes it’s not even that the material is hard — it’s just that I didn’t really stay with it. It’s more the other way around, I think — I’m at the museum, someone is talking directly to me about the next art walk or something we’re planning, and I’m looking at them like I’m listening. Like I caught the beginning, and maybe I catch the end, but there’s this gap where I don’t really know how we got from one point to the next. That’s when I get uncomfortable, because I have to say, “I’m sorry, can you run that by me again?” And I don’t like doing that, especially if they were explaining something pretty straightforward.\n\nSo the anxious or embarrassed feeling is more after I notice I missed it, not before. Beforehand it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're alone, like when you're trying to work on a project by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely when I’m alone too. In some ways it’s easier to notice then, because there isn’t another person there keeping me on track.\n\nLike if I sit down with a gallery packet or a set of notes, I can start out fine. I’ll think, okay, I’m just going to read through this and get what I need from it. And for the first few minutes I usually do. But then my eyes are still moving over the page, like I’m reading each line, and my mind has kind of wandered off somewhere else without me realizing it.\n\nThen I get to the end of a paragraph or a section and I have that moment of, “Wait, what did I just read?” And I’ll have to go back and read the same part again. Sometimes it’s not even that the material is hard — it’s just that I didn’t really stay with it. That can happen even when I’m by myself and there’s nothing especially dramatic going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these episodes of zoning out or missing parts of conversations are affecting your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I don’t always like admitting that, because I’ve been involved with the museum group for a long time and I used to feel pretty steady with it.\n\nIt’s not like anyone has sat me down and said, “You can’t handle this,” or anything harsh like that. It’s been more subtle. Some of the planning pieces — like keeping track of what needs to be discussed for an art walk, or following through on little details from a meeting — those have sort of quietly shifted away from me. Someone else will take the lead on that part now, or they’ll say, “Oh, I’ve got this,” and I can tell it’s partly because I’ve missed things or needed things repeated.\n\nAnd that does affect how I feel leading the art walks. I used to feel confident being out front, talking through the pieces and keeping the flow going. Now I second-guess myself more. I worry I’m going to miss something someone told me, or forget part of the plan, or not be as on top of it as I should be. So I still care about it, very much, but I don’t feel as reliable in that role as I used to.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or upset do you feel about these episodes of zoning out and missing parts of conversations?\n\nPatient: It bothers me, yes. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart over it every day, but it does upset me more than I probably let on.\n\nThe hardest part is that it’s changed how I feel in the museum group. That’s been a place where I used to feel useful and, you know, like I had my part. But because I miss pieces of conversations or don’t catch all the details, some of the planning things have sort of been moved away from me. Not in a mean way — nobody has been cruel about it — but I notice it. Someone else will step in to handle a detail, or they’ll kind of take over the organizing part, and I’m left thinking, “Well, they probably don’t trust me with that like they used to.”\n\nAnd leading the art walks… that’s where I feel it emotionally. I used to feel pretty confident doing that. Now I get this nagging feeling that I’m going to miss something important or not be as sharp as I should be. So the upset is not constant panic or anything like that, but it’s discouraging. It makes me feel less capable than I used to feel.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing everyday tasks because of these episodes of zoning out?\n\nPatient: Yes, some. Not every single day, and I do eventually get things done most of the time, but the follow-through is where I notice it.\n\nFor example, with the volunteer group, someone might give me a few things to handle before an art walk. And I’ll start out doing it — like I’ll print the maps, and I’ll feel like, okay, I’m on it. But then the next part, like calling the museum desk or making sure the folders are completely ready, can just sort of slip away from me. It’s not that I decided not to do it. It’s more like I did the first piece and my brain treated that as if the whole task was finished.\n\nThen later someone will ask, “Did you call the desk?” or “Are the folders ready?” and I’ll realize they’re only half-ready, or I never made the call. That’s embarrassing, because these are ordinary things. They’re not complicated. But if there are several steps, especially if nobody checks in, I can lose track after the first part and need a reminder.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yes, I’d say it does, though maybe not in the sense of, you know, I can’t get through a normal day at home. It’s more that the things that give structure to my week — especially the museum group — have changed because of it.

I’ve noticed that some of the planning parts have sort of quietly been taken off my plate. Nobody makes a big announcement about it, but I can tell. If there’s something that needs organizing before an art walk, someone else will step in or handle the details now, where before I would’ve been trusted to keep track of it. And I understand why, because I have missed pieces of conversations or not followed through the way I meant to, but it still stings.

And with leading the art walks, that’s probably where it affects my routine the most emotionally. I still show up, and I still care about doing it well, but I don’t feel confident the way I used to. I find myself worrying that I’m going to miss part of what was planned or not have the flow right. So yes, it’s significant to me — maybe mild compared to some people, but in my daily life, or at least my regular week, it has definitely made me feel less steady and less useful than before.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is… it’s kind of embarrassing, actually. I’ve been noticing that when people are talking directly to me, like at the museum when we’re discussing the next art walk, I’ll be looking right at them and nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I missed the whole middle part of what they said. Not because I don’t care—I do care—but it’s like my mind slips off somewhere for a moment.\n\nThen I have to ask them to repeat it, like, “Could you run that by me again?” And after a while I feel awkward, because it’s not like they were across the room or mumbling. They were right there in front of me. So I guess I’m here because that keeps happening, and I’d like to understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do these moments where your mind slips away tend to happen more often when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I mean… I haven’t really sorted out whether it’s stress exactly. It happens in pretty ordinary situations too, like at the museum when we’re talking about the next art walk. Someone will be speaking right to me, and I’ll be looking at them, nodding like I’m following, and then all of a sudden I realize I didn’t catch the middle of what they said.\n\nAnd then I have to say, “Sorry, could you run that by me again?” It’s not that I’m panicking in that moment or anything. It’s more like my attention just sort of drifts off without me noticing, and by the time I notice, I’ve missed a chunk of it. So stress might make it worse, maybe, but I can’t say it only happens when I’m anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened earlier in the day or things that people told you outside of these moments when your mind slips away?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate that out, to be honest. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who just can’t remember the day or anything like that. If something happened earlier, I usually have some sense of it.\n\nBut with things people tell me… that’s harder to answer, because sometimes if I don’t remember it later, I don’t always know whether I forgot it after hearing it, or whether I never really took it in properly in the first place. Like, if someone says, “Remember I told you this?” I may think, well, maybe you did, but maybe that was one of those times where I was looking like I was listening and I missed part of it.\n\nSo outside of those moments, I haven’t especially noticed a separate memory problem, if that makes sense. It feels more like the trouble starts at the point of taking in what’s being said.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like a sensation in your body, when your mind slips away and you miss part of what someone is saying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. It’s not like I feel dizzy or my heart starts racing or I get a tightness in my chest right when it happens. At least, I haven’t connected it with anything like that.\n\nIt’s more that afterward I sort of “come back,” if that’s the right way to put it, and realize I’ve missed something. Then I feel awkward or a little embarrassed because the person was talking right to me and I was acting like I was following. But physically, during the slipping-away part, I can’t say there’s a specific sensation I recognize. If there is, it’s subtle enough that I haven’t really picked up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself zoning out or missing parts of what you're reading or watching, similar to what happens when you're listening to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens too. Reading is probably the easiest example for me to notice, because there’s a page right in front of me showing that I didn’t really take it in.\n\nLike, if I sit down with a gallery packet, or some notes about an exhibit or an upcoming art walk, I’ll start out okay. I’ll be reading the first paragraph or two and thinking, alright, I’m following this. But then after a few minutes my eyes are still going across the page, line by line, and it looks like I’m reading, but my mind has gone somewhere else. Then I get to the bottom of a section and realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I’ll go back and read the same part again. Sometimes I have to do that more than once, which gets a little frustrating because it’s not usually that the material is impossible or anything. It’s more like I didn’t stay with it long enough for it to land. Watching something can be similar, but with reading I notice it more clearly because I catch myself rereading the same few lines.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself zoning out or losing focus when you're doing tasks that require your full attention, like working on a project or during conversations with multiple people?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, especially with things like reading through a gallery packet or a set of notes where I really do need to take it in. I’ll sit down and start out perfectly fine — like the first little bit, I’m with it, I understand what I’m reading, I know why I’m looking at it.\n\nBut then after a few minutes, it’s like my eyes keep doing the job but my mind doesn’t. I’m still moving across the page, line by line, and if someone looked at me they’d probably think I was reading carefully. But then I get to the end of a paragraph or a section and realize, wait, I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nSo I have to go back and read the same part again, sometimes more than once. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid it, and it’s not that I can’t understand the material. It’s more like my attention just doesn’t stay attached to it long enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset right before your mind slips away and you miss part of what someone is saying?\n\nPatient: Not really right before, no. I wouldn’t say I feel anxious first and then drift off. It’s more the other way around, I think — I’m at the museum, someone is talking directly to me about the next art walk or something we’re planning, and I’m looking at them like I’m listening. I’m not upset in that moment, not that I can tell.\n\nThen all of a sudden I realize I missed the middle of what they said. Like I caught the beginning, and maybe I catch the end, but there’s this gap where I don’t really know how we got from one point to the next. That’s when I get uncomfortable, because I have to say, “I’m sorry, can you run that by me again?” And I don’t like doing that, especially if they were explaining something pretty straightforward.\n\nSo the anxious or embarrassed feeling is more after I notice I missed it, not before. Beforehand it just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're alone, like when you're trying to work on a project by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely when I’m alone too. In some ways it’s easier to notice then, because there isn’t another person there keeping me on track.\n\nLike if I sit down with a gallery packet or a set of notes, I can start out fine. I’ll think, okay, I’m just going to read through this and get what I need from it. And for the first few minutes I usually do. But then my eyes are still moving over the page, like I’m reading each line, and my mind has kind of wandered off somewhere else without me realizing it.\n\nThen I get to the end of a paragraph or a section and I have that moment of, “Wait, what did I just read?” And I’ll have to go back and read the same part again. Sometimes it’s not even that the material is hard — it’s just that I didn’t really stay with it. That can happen even when I’m by myself and there’s nothing especially dramatic going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these episodes of zoning out or missing parts of conversations are affecting your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I don’t always like admitting that, because I’ve been involved with the museum group for a long time and I used to feel pretty steady with it.\n\nIt’s not like anyone has sat me down and said, “You can’t handle this,” or anything harsh like that. It’s been more subtle. Some of the planning pieces — like keeping track of what needs to be discussed for an art walk, or following through on little details from a meeting — those have sort of quietly shifted away from me. Someone else will take the lead on that part now, or they’ll say, “Oh, I’ve got this,” and I can tell it’s partly because I’ve missed things or needed things repeated.\n\nAnd that does affect how I feel leading the art walks. I used to feel confident being out front, talking through the pieces and keeping the flow going. Now I second-guess myself more. I worry I’m going to miss something someone told me, or forget part of the plan, or not be as on top of it as I should be. So I still care about it, very much, but I don’t feel as reliable in that role as I used to.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or upset do you feel about these episodes of zoning out and missing parts of conversations?\n\nPatient: It bothers me, yes. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart over it every day, but it does upset me more than I probably let on.\n\nThe hardest part is that it’s changed how I feel in the museum group. That’s been a place where I used to feel useful and, you know, like I had my part. But because I miss pieces of conversations or don’t catch all the details, some of the planning things have sort of been moved away from me. Not in a mean way — nobody has been cruel about it — but I notice it. Someone else will step in to handle a detail, or they’ll kind of take over the organizing part, and I’m left thinking, “Well, they probably don’t trust me with that like they used to.”\n\nAnd leading the art walks… that’s where I feel it emotionally. I used to feel pretty confident doing that. Now I get this nagging feeling that I’m going to miss something important or not be as sharp as I should be. So the upset is not constant panic or anything like that, but it’s discouraging. It makes me feel less capable than I used to feel.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing everyday tasks because of these episodes of zoning out?\n\nPatient: Yes, some. Not every single day, and I do eventually get things done most of the time, but the follow-through is where I notice it.\n\nFor example, with the volunteer group, someone might give me a few things to handle before an art walk. And I’ll start out doing it — like I’ll print the maps, and I’ll feel like, okay, I’m on it. But then the next part, like calling the museum desk or making sure the folders are completely ready, can just sort of slip away from me. It’s not that I decided not to do it. It’s more like I did the first piece and my brain treated that as if the whole task was finished.\n\nThen later someone will ask, “Did you call the desk?” or “Are the folders ready?” and I’ll realize they’re only half-ready, or I never made the call. That’s embarrassing, because these are ordinary things. They’re not complicated. But if there are several steps, especially if nobody checks in, I can lose track after the first part and need a reminder.\n\nDoctor: Are these episodes of zoning out affecting your daily routine in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it does, though maybe not in the sense of, you know, I can’t get through a normal day at home. It’s more that the things that give structure to my week — especially the museum group — have changed because of it.\n\nI’ve noticed that some of the planning parts have sort of quietly been taken off my plate. Nobody makes a big announcement about it, but I can tell. If there’s something that needs organizing before an art walk, someone else will step in or handle the details now, where before I would’ve been trusted to keep track of it. And I understand why, because I have missed pieces of conversations or not followed through the way I meant to, but it still stings.\n\nAnd with leading the art walks, that’s probably where it affects my routine the most emotionally. I still show up, and I still care about doing it well, but I don’t feel confident the way I used to. I find myself worrying that I’m going to miss part of what was planned or not have the flow right. So yes, it’s significant to me — maybe mild compared to some people, but in my daily life, or at least my regular week, it has definitely made me feel less steady and less useful than before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ve been noticing that when people are talking directly to me, like at the museum when we’re discussing the next art walk, I’ll be looking right at them and nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I missed the whole middle part of what they said. Not because I don’t care—I do care—but it’s like my mind slips off somewhere for a moment.\n\nThen I have to ask them to repeat it, like, “Could you run that by me again?” And after a while I feel awkward, because it’s not like they were across the room or mumbling. They were right there in front of me. So I guess I’m here because that keeps happening, and I’d like to understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do these moments where your mind slips away tend to happen more often when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I mean… I haven’t really sorted out whether it’s stress exactly. It happens in pretty ordinary situations too, like at the museum when we’re talking about the next art walk. Someone will be speaking right to me, and I’ll be looking at them, nodding like I’m following, and then all of a sudden I realize I didn’t catch the middle of what they said.\n\nAnd then I have to say, “Sorry, could you run that by me again?” It’s not that I’m panicking in that moment or anything. It’s more like my attention just sort of drifts off without me noticing, and by the time I notice, I’ve missed a chunk of it. So stress might make it worse, maybe, but I can’t say it only happens when I’m anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened earlier in the day or things that people told you outside of these moments when your mind slips away?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate that out, to be honest. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who just can’t remember the day or anything like that.
